(2) Aurelion cites Broadcom saying "optical scale-up is not quite there yet," alongside Jensen's copper-for-scale-up / photonics-for-scale-out framing, as grounds that the copper-runs-out mechanism is "slightly misguided." (3) The integration threat, and this is Aurelion's own view rather than management's: they call Lumentum's telecom-survivorship defence "the weakest argument… Broadcom and Marvell have plenty of money, and they don't need the best laser in the world. They only need one that is good enough to put on their own chip." PhotonCap frames the dual role precisely — "Broadcom is a potential customer and at the same time a platform competitor with its own laser sources," while "Marvell can be a buyer, an ecosystem partner, and an integration layer competitor all at once. Absent a disclosed design win, though, these are demand paths, not confirmed customers."** ⚠️ **No design win, no revenue figure, no dated program — a competitive-posture datapoint, not a modelling input, and deliberately kept to one row rather than expanded into a thesis.** Ties to the open Citi 07-16 question on [COHR.md](COHR.md) of whether Broadcom sources CPO components from Coherent or self-integrates. | 2026-07-30 | ⭐ AMD | Aurelion Research / PhotonCap "A Conversation with Lumentum" (2026-07-29). 1 intra-quarter row (07-29, produto/mixed) + 1 Sources entry. NOTHING SUPERSEDED — no rating, PT, estimate or existing AMD number changed.** ⚠️ **Logged as a READ-THROUGH, and specifically as PhotonCap's independent technical commentary — not AMD management, not Lumentum management. The subject company is Lumentum; the source is a joint independent publication (management meeting + investment analysis by Aurelion Research, technical comments by PhotonCap), not a bank.** **Two marks, both about network intensity rather than accelerator share. (1) The AMD–Cerebras July split-inference architecture is cited as the live proof that inference is becoming network-heavy: "Helios handles the front half, reading the prompt, and Cerebras handles the back half, generating the answer, so work in progress crosses a network between two machines." ⚠️ PhotonCap bounds it immediately and the bound is retained verbatim because it is the honest part: "What that link is made of has not been disclosed. So this is evidence that inference moves a lot of data, not evidence of any vendor's revenue." It is offered against Aurelion's own caution that "inference is less network intensive… so a mix shift toward inference does not translate to a 1:1 demand for optics," with the gap closing via longer context windows, heavier routing, disaggregated prefill/decode and agentic usage. (2) "Every camp pursuing CPO needs high power light. AMD's rack scale expansion creates EML, CW, and pluggable demand… Absent a disclosed design win, though, these are demand paths, not confirmed customers" — i.e. AMD's rack-scale roadmap as an incremental optical-demand source, with no supplier, design win or dated program attached.** Consistent with the MI500 NPO + ELSFP architecture already on this page from SemiAnalysis (07-24). **Deliberately kept to one row rather than expanded into a thesis.** Cross-filed [LITE.md](LITE.md), [COHR.md](COHR.md), [themes/optical-cpo.md](themes/optical-cpo.md). |

Two sources already on this page say the opposite about SoIC: Redburn (Conor O'Mara, 06-23) describes an "aggressive SOIC expansion 2027-28" with new silicon-photonics and Broadcom-next-gen-TPU use cases, and Susquehanna (Hosseini, 07-11) has "SoIC ramping meaningfully through 2028." Fubon now says SoIC is being deliberately deprioritised to feed CoWoS. Both readings are on the page; neither is retired. The 2027 customer allocation is NVDA 50.0% / 945,000 wafers · AVGO 22.3% / 421,800 · AWS 10.4% / 196,500 · MediaTek 7.3% / 137,700 · AMD 7.0% / 132,300 · MSFT 1.0% / 18,900 · XLNX and CSCO 0.5% / 9,450 each · Others 1.0% / 18,900.** **⚠️ Unit-basis guardrail recorded so this is not misread downstream, the same class of error the UBS package-vs-die note caused on 07-27: these are TSMC-ONLY CoWoS wafers. Fubon separately quotes TOTAL allocations that include non-TSMC packaging — NVDA at 1.03mn in 2027 vs 629k in 2026 (TSMC + SPIL, the SPIL leg for Vera CPU) and AMD at 300k with the majority of the increase from ASE, not TSMC. | 2026-07-28 | ⭐ SKHYNIX | **/run-inbox (MS memory webcast, 2026-07-28 — recorded PRE-print). 1 intra-quarter row, 2 Current-state blocks, 2 Sinal cells updated, Síntese extended, 1 Source. No rating, PT or estimate superseded.** **Three things this adds to the Q2 print already on the page.** **(1) The bogey, which sharpens the "miss against a stale consensus" defence into something more precise: MS's Sean had the buyside range at ₩60-65tn with "our number… currently at 65 trillion" and "some adjustment towards the 60 handle."** So the **₩60.54tn** actual landed at the *bottom* of the range and **~7% below MS's own number** — the sell-side had cut, MS had not, and the screen consensus of ₩64.2tn was the lagging figure. **(2) His stated test, which is the better scoring rule and worth keeping as a template: "the number itself will not be that meaningful. If tomorrow we do have numbers and stocks bouncing, I think that would be good news for the sector."** Scored against the tape — **Hynix opened -6% and recovered to -2.3% while [[MU]] fell ~8.9% intraday plus ~4% after hours** — the result is **ambiguous-to-mildly-constructive for the name, negative for the complex**; not the clean bounce, not the rout. **(3) The genuinely net-new analytical point, and it inverts the sign on the page's central negative: Sean names "Hynix probably HBM3E, where no HBM4 will actually reduce the amount of total HBM and more DRAM will be produced as a result" as one driver of TrendForce industry DRAM bit growth moving from "a 25% type of grow to now reaching 31%" this year (~24% next).** The same HBM4 slip that caused the revenue miss is **adding commodity DRAM bits to the industry** at a moment when commodity-DRAM returns are competitive with HBM (this page's own HBM-vs-EPS paradox, MS/Sean Kim 04-14). Also added: **the buyback was an expected catalyst that did not arrive** ("it could happen, given the current volatility, but again, we have no visibility on that," against a buyside pricing "the potential buyback as near-term catalyst"), now marked as a resolved miss in the Capex/capital-return Sinal row; and Sean's **CXMT-as-transfer framing** — a **US$492bn** debut close, 25th most valuable company globally, "quite proportionate to the amount of market cap that was taken away from stocks like Hynix," with fundamental impact bounded to the outer years and **the 2030 expansion pace as the real question**. ⚠️ **Data-integrity flag raised, not adopted: the webcast's "CXMT currently at about 30,000 wafers capacity" cannot be reconciled with the ~290-350k wspm figures this page and [[MU]] carry from Wells Fargo (07-14) and BofA (07-12); treated as a transcription/sub-segment artifact.** ⚠️ **Date tension noted for the record: the webcast is filed 2026-07-28 but is internally pre-print ("earnings for Hynix tomorrow… Samsung on Thursday" = 07-30), while this page dates the Q2 print 2026-07-28.** The commentary is unambiguously pre-print and has been labelled as such throughout; the one-day filing discrepancy does not affect any figure. Cross-filed [[MU]], [[SAMSUNG]], [[SNDK]]. |
| 2026-07-28 | ⭐ SKHYNIX | **/wiki-ingest · Q2 2026 PRINT (2026-07-28) — new-print reset.** New `## Current state` for the June quarter (the Q1 2026 block plus all the standing capacity/HBM4/MR-MUF/packaging deep-dive material is retained inline below it); archived the Q1 2026 intra-quarter window (Apr 23 → Jul 28, **130 lines** — the largest window on the wiki) into this Changelog; opened a fresh Q2 2026 window; rolled the management-evolution table forward (Q2 2025 drops off, **Q2 2026 added**). **Superseded per the thesis-drift rule** — prior values preserved: *Current state* previously led with **Q1 2026 revenue ₩52.6tn (+60% q/q, +198% y/y), operating profit ₩37.6tn, OP margin 72%, net profit ₩40.3tn, EBITDA ₩41.3tn, net cash ₩35tn.** **What printed: revenue ₩79.3tn vs ~₩83.9tn expected and operating profit ₩60.54tn vs ₩64.2tn consensus — the first headline miss of the cycle.** ⚠️ **The qualifier matters as much as the miss: local Korean brokers had already cut OP to ~₩60tn and Jefferies' Jeff Kim was at ₩62tn**, so the print is in line with the most recent numbers and only misses the stale screen consensus. Margin structure intact — **GM 83%, OM 76%, EBITDA margin 81%** — and the *level* still rose sequentially off Q1. **The margin trajectory this page has tracked (41% → 47% → 58% → 72%) therefore breaks its ascent at 76%.** **Cause of the shortfall, per Jefferies: the HBM4 ramp is late** — HBM4 shipments did begin in Q2 at customer-required speeds and **1cnm HBM4E samples went to a major customer in 1H**, but the ramp slipped relative to plan. Other management detail logged: **DRAM Q2 bits +HSD with ASP +30% q/q, 2026 bit growth guided mid-20s%, Q3 bits +10% server-focused**; **SOCAMM2 fully commercialised on 1C**; demand **broadening as AI evolves into agentic form** across HBM/server DRAM/eSSD; **PC and smartphone softness explicitly attributed to difficulty securing memory volumes, not weak end-demand.** **Two developments change the shape of the debate rather than the numbers.** **(1) The [[NVDA]] anchor is being re-read as a pricing constraint.** Jefferies expects the miss to fuel the view that **"Hynix has been less aggressive than [[SAMSUNG]] on pricing given its commitments to NVDA on HBM"** — i.e. the supplier-differentiation moat this page underwrites may also be a price ceiling. **(2) Capex is stepping up ~80% to the high-₩40tn range and is being pulled in, with no buyback despite large net cash** (Culpium; Jefferies) — landing in the same week Jefferies research published **"HAS MEMORY PEAKED?": price hikes tracking 15-20% vs 25-30% expected, memory prices peaking ~1H27, and 2027 consensus requiring ~US$2tn of US AI capex to be achievable**, with **HBM4 costs jumping to US$31-32/GB**. That is the bear case in one line — peak-margin cash committed to capacity into a forecast price peak. It is also consistent with management's own long-standing argument that ~70% OP margins are "not high enough" to fund the greenfield/hybrid-bonding step-up. **Tape context (not a company event, but it dominated the reaction):** on 2026-07-27 the **KOSPI fell 10.7% with a sell sidecar and the year's eighth circuit breaker, Samsung -12%, SK Hynix -13%**, driven by **CXMT's IPO debut at +466% — China's second-largest IPO ever — "making the memory threat feel suddenly very real"** plus a live-Fed-hike scare (~40% odds priced). On the print the stock opened **-6% pre-market and recovered to -2.3%**, while **[[MU]] fell a further ~4% after hours on top of -8.9% intraday**. Also logged: an unverified @zerohedge claim that SK Hynix slumped below a US IPO price — **flagged, not adopted**, as it is uncorroborated elsewhere in this ingest; and a Korean-press exclusive (@jukan05) that **LPDDR6 mass production starts in 2H with Xiaomi as first customer**, which sits in tension with the same-day comment that PC/mobile volumes are being rationed. Cross-filed [[MU]], [[SAMSUNG]], [[NVDA]], [themes/hbm-memory](themes/hbm-memory.md), [themes/china-export](themes/china-export.md). |

So 70k is the live mark, 90k the superseded one — Beavington was right, the Wong relay inverted.** The cause is stated too: **[[TSM]] is slowing SoIC expansion to prioritise CoWoS**, which is also why Fubon flags **potential upside to its 220kwpm end-2028 CoWoS number** (end-2027 held at 180kwpm). **A SECOND relay error corrected on the same row: the interposer counts are inverted. Fubon says "12 and 10 interposers per CoWoS wafer for Rubin and Rubin Ultra, respectively" — Rubin 12, Rubin Ultra 10, not the reverse.** The conclusion is unchanged (Rubin Ultra's interposer only marginally larger post-cancellation → chip output 12.4mn 2027 vs 8.2mn 2026), but the numbers on the page were backwards and are now annotated in place rather than deleted. **Net-new #1, and it needs its own guardrail: NVDA's TOTAL capacity allocation is 1.03mn wafers in 2027 vs 629k in 2026 — this is TSMC + SPIL and is BROADER than the TSMC-CoWoS-only line of 945,000 wafers (50.0% of 1.89mn; 586,500 / 50.1% in 2026; 346,800 / 51.4% in 2025). The ~85k gap is SPIL capacity supporting Vera CPU. Do not net the two.** **Net-new #2, a relative-advantage datapoint the page did not have: HBM4 costs NVDA US$31-32/GB (from US$17-18/GB HBM3e in 2026), while "other GPU and custom-ASIC projects… may reach US$35-36 per GB, meaning the HBM cost will double in 2027." HBM inflation is real for NVDA (~+80%) but it buys ~10-15% cheaper than the merchant/ASIC field — a scale edge that partly offsets the ASIC-TCO bear this page carries from JPM/UBS.** On that BOM Fubon models the **Rubin ASP at US$78-80K on a 75-80% chip GM** (confirmed against the Beavington relay, not re-entered). **Net-new #3 — the fullest account on this page of what replaces Kyber, folded into the Product/Rubin Signal cell: Kyber was cancelled/delayed in MARCH/APRIL on mid-plane bandwidth issues; it carried 576 compute dies per rack (144 GPUs × 4 compute dies); checks say NVDA still targets 576 in the replacement, via either (a) four Oberon racks per system on traditional scale-out (144 compute dies each) or (b) a two-rack "Taycan" design at 144 GPUs × 2 compute dies per rack with NPO/CPO for the inter-rack scale-out and cable retained for intra-rack scale-up. Fubon explicitly declines to name winners and losers because neither is finalised — but says "nVidia is still aggressive in the NPO/CPO solution despite a lot of market noise."** Positioning kept in Fubon's own words on both sides: short-term the **NVDA supply chain "could potentially underperform due to Rubin's lower-than-expected volume in 2H26 and uncertain design change in the next generation rack design,"** with Fubon preferring the CPU/SPE/TPU chains — against **"token cost is still a more important factor in driving CSP's capex, and we remain positive about AI market demand."** **Two secondary sources added as separate rows. (1) MS "The Paths to 25-50% GenAI ROIC" (Nowak/Byrd/Wood, 07-27) — logged explicitly as a demand-durability read-through and NOT as an NVDA mark (the note has no NVDA rating or PT): MS standardises all three GenAI unit-economics frameworks on 1GW of NVDA GB300 (410,256 chips/GW, US$39bn all-in capex/GW = US$23bn IT on 5yr + US$16bn non-IT on 15yr, ~US$4mn/GB300 rack, 75% utilisation, US$8.50/hr, 2,750 tokens/sec/GPU) and gets ~31% ROIC / ~67% incremental EBIT margin on hyperscaler GPU rental, ~46% / ~75% on a model-enabled API on owned infra, ~25% / ~31% on 3P infra. The buy-side point is that the returns math on the CURRENT generation clears a normal hurdle at every layer — the most direct available answer to the Crisafulli/Chanos/Burry "hyperscalers can't generate FCF" bear. MS's own caveat kept: custom ASICs and prior GPU generations have lower costs, but "chip pricing/hour and token throughput are also lower."** **(2) MS TMT webcast (07-28): Joe Moore's rank order across his coverage is NVDA > AVGO > MU > SNDK — "nothing else that's wildly compelling" — on the argument that the four are "just so much cheaper than every other name that has some speculative potential"; and Lee Simpson names NVIDIA specifically as a user of Cadence's agentic-EDA offering (a design-productivity datapoint, monetisation "really a 2027 story," not folded into any estimate).** Cross-filed [[TSM]], [[AVGO]], [[AMD]], [[MEDIATEK]], [[MU]], [[CDNS]], [themes/cowos-packaging](themes/cowos-packaging.md), [themes/hbm-memory](themes/hbm-memory.md). |

Logged with the caution MS attaches: **larger models price higher but throughput lower, so higher token pricing does NOT scale revenue/GW proportionately** — which is a direct argument against assuming a frontier-parity Muse would earn proportionately more, and it sits alongside MS's own 07-01 markdown of Muse on TerminalBench/SWE-Bench (unchanged). ⚠️ **THE CONTRADICTION WORTH THE MOST, AND IT IS INSIDE THE BULL'S OWN MODEL — logged, not smoothed: MS's key-earnings-inputs table has GAAP operating income at 83,276 (2025) → 92,216 (26e) → 101,748 (27e) → 104,841 (28e), i.e. +10.7% / +10.3% / ONLY +3.0%, against constant-currency revenue growth of 22.5 → 26.0 → 22.0 → 19.8%.** Revenue compounding near 20% while operating income flat-lines at +3% is the depreciation wall from MS's own **$225bn/$250bn '27/'28 capex path** (already on this page from 07-12) arriving in the P&L. **That is the "incremental ROIC deeply negative" bear from the 06-11 BofA call, expressed in the top-pick analyst's published estimates — the $775 and the $104.8bn of 2028 operating income are held together only by call options MS states are not in the base numbers.** Corroborating: MS is **above** consensus on FY26e revenue (254,653 vs 253,077) and EBITDA (148,034 vs 142,872) but **below** on EPS (32.49 vs 32.95) — the heavier depreciation/interest load showing through. NO RATING OR PT SUPERSEDED — MS reiterates Overweight, PT $400** (Nowak's 07-23 post-print mark), and Barclays $405, BofA $430, DB $430, Citi $447, GS $450 and JPM $460 all stand. **⚠️ ONE THESIS DRIFT, AND IT IS A HOUSE REVERSING ITSELF: Fubon's 2026-07-08 conclusion that Google would "ultimately rely on TSMC's 2028 CoWoS expansion" for TPU V9 is SUPERSEDED by Fubon's own 2026-07-28 note — "it is difficult to reach Google's target with TSMC alone, and Intel's supply is a must by 2028."** The 07-08 text is **retained verbatim in the TPU/merchant-silicon section as the dated prior mark**, annotated as superseded, and recorded here for the record. **The reversal is driven by demand, not by an improved yield view, and that is the part to carry: "Google plans to have 12-15mn TPUs in 2028. Entering 2028, Google's TPUs will enter the V9 generation with FOUR COMPUTE DIES, suggesting that their capacity consumption will MORE THAN DOUBLE in 2028 vs. 2027"** — a four-die package does not fit inside TSMC's book at that volume, regardless of what EMIB-T yields do. Fubon concedes the yield doubt it raised on 07-08 is unanswered ("still a lot of noise on Intel's EMIB-T progress, especially its learning curve improvement") but is now constructive on Intel *capacity*: **EMIB 10-12k/month by end-2026 doubling to 24-25k/month by end-2027**, CFO earmarking a great portion of new capex for advanced packaging; Ibiden's next call named as the tell. TSMC baseline added: **CoWoS 180kwpm end-2027 → 220kwpm end-2028**, with SoIC 4Q28 cut from 90k to 70k to prioritise CoWoS, and ASE FoCoS raised to 50kwpm. **Effect on the page's standing three-way v9 disagreement: it narrows to 2-vs-1 for EMIB-T** — Fubon crosses to join **UBS Asia (07-27)**, leaving **JPM · Gokul (07-08, "stays on CoWoS," Qualcomm/MediaTek-packaged)** as the holdout, while **UBS's own Arcuri (SemiBytes, 07-27) still calls EMIB-T "very low yields and immaturity."** **No view adopted; the divergence is narrower, not resolved.** ⚠️ **Second flag, deliberately left open: a basis conflict on the same 12-15mn number from the same house.** Jefferies · Sherman Chang relayed it on 07-17 (post-TSMC-2Q call with Fubon) as a **chip/die-basis** figure — "based on chips" — and Fubon now restates it as "12-15mn TPUs" against a V9 carrying **four compute dies per package**. Those cannot both be right at face value (12-15mn dies ≈ 3-4mn packages; 12-15mn packages ≈ 48-60mn dies). Both readings kept verbatim; for calibration UBS's 07-27 EMIB-T model has **2.3mn Google chipsets in 2028** at four XPUs per package, which is consistent with the package count being low-single-digit millions. **Third, and the sharpest cross-check against this page's own equity narrative: Fubon calls Google's 2028 target "very aggressive" EXPLICITLY "despite market concerns on Google capex and negative FCF"** — i.e. the packaging supply chain is being told to build for a plan the equity market punished with a -7.8% week on the first negative-FCF quarter. That is the supply-side/equity-side divergence stated by a source that has no equity axe on GOOGL (its top TPU pick is MediaTek). **On the MS note: the ROIC framework is the material addition, not the PT.** All three models standardise on **1GW of GB300 (410,256 chips, $39bn all-in capex/GW, ~$6bn/yr depreciation, 75% utilisation, $7.6bn/GW opex, 21% tax)**, and **GOOGL is the only name MS puts on both sides** — bullish "hyperscalers AMZN, GOOGL, MSFT" *and* "model developers GOOGL and META." GCP leg (**~67% incremental EBIT margin, ~31% ROIC** at $8.50/hr) plus Gemini leg (**~75% margin, ~46% ROIC** on owned infra at 2,750 tok/s/GPU and $1.75/mn) is the first published statement that Alphabet captures both the landlord and the tenant spread on the same gigawatt — the quantified form of the vertical-integration argument Google's own Principal Architect made on 06-23. The rented-infra framework (**~25% NOPAT margin** at $7.75/hr) prices the ~$50/W SpaceX arrangement as a bridge cost. **Two caveats logged rather than buried:** MS states reported near-term incremental margins run **below** the band (prior-gen mix, traditional-cloud mix, unmodelled opex), so this does not contradict the 35.6% reported Cloud OI margin; and MS explicitly notes the frameworks are **GB300-based** — "while custom ASICs and prior GPU generation have lower costs… we believe chip pricing/hour and token throughput are also lower" — so **these are not TPU economics and should not be quoted as such**. Risk-reward detail added (all net-new except the $400): bull **$450**, bear **$225**, the **~1.6x PEG / ~35% peer-median premium** framing, **spot $319.74**, mean consensus PT **$420.48** (range $220-$515), **89% OW / 11% EW**, options-implied **P(>$400) ~23.5% vs P(<$225) ~15.3%** — **the only one of MS's four internet names where the base case is more likely than the bear**, which reads the 07-22 drawdown as capex de-rating rather than thesis impairment. Estimates: total GAAP operating income **129,039 → 176,395 → 227,461 → 280,882** (+36.7%/+29.0%/+23.5%, the sharpest contrast on the complex with META's +3.0% 2028 OI growth on a comparable capex build); MS vs cons FY26e revenue **500,611 vs 495,765**, EBITDA **241,667 vs 232,729**, net income **255,100 vs 234,369**, EPS **20.62 vs 19.37**. Two anomalies logged, not corrected: MS shows **two operating-income series** in the same table (129,039→280,882 and 137,054→287,432), and its FY26e **net income exceeds its own EBITDA** (255,100 vs 241,667) — plausibly the Anthropic revaluation BofA models (07-16), but MS does not label it. House cross-check: MS's **$15 '27 EPS sits below the Capstone model's $16.20** (2026-06-05), so the house stays above MS on the out-year. Cross-filed [[MSFT]], [[AMZN]], [[META]], [[MEDIATEK]], [[AVGO]], [[INTC]], [themes/custom-asic-tpu](themes/custom-asic-tpu.md), [themes/hyperscaler-capex](themes/hyperscaler-capex.md). |

what changed is the price.** Also logged from the same BofA note (not ASML-specific, kept for the read-across): after a fireside with STM management, BofA's conviction on the data-centre story is rising — demand ahead of supply, capacity expanding at **Crolles 2 and Agrate**, and STM's **>$2bn 2028 data-centre target "increasingly appears conservative."** Cross-filed [[AMAT]], [[LRCX]], [[KLAC]], [[SMIC]], [themes/china-export](themes/china-export.md), [themes/semicap-wfe](themes/semicap-wfe.md). |

the **+6% GM frame is a two-year-old "opportunity space," not guidance, and "things are looking a little better"** with new numbers due at the **CMD in June 2027**; immersion carries ASML's **best margins of all product lines**; | 2026-07-28 | ⭐ AAPL | **/wiki-ingest (Outlook). 1 intra-quarter row. No rating/PT superseded** (BofA Buy $380 reiterated). **Apple formally launched “Apple Upgrade” in the US** — a hardware-**leasing** platform spanning iPhone, Watch, Mac and iPad, sold through the online store, the Apple Store app and retail, and **financed via Klarna**; 12/24-month terms on iPhone and Watch, 24/36-month on Mac and iPad, from **$17.99/mo (iPhone)**. **The mechanism is the point, and it is a genuine change to how Apple captures value from the installed base rather than a new financing SKU.** BofA's articulation: under purchase/installment, Apple monetises the device fully up front but **the customer owns the residual**, which Apple only recaptures if the customer elects to trade in; under a lease, the customer pays less per month and **returns the device**, so **Apple monetises the residual itself**. The net incremental is therefore **lease payments + returned-device value + a faster replacement cycle**, with secondary benefits from **lower affordability friction, premium mix, and greater direct control of the customer relationship** — the last being strategically consistent with Apple's long-running push to disintermediate carriers. BofA is careful that the underlying mechanics (trade-ins, monthly financing, the iPhone Upgrade Program) are not new; **what is new is packaging them into one platform across all four major hardware categories.** Logged as additive product/strategy evidence for the installed-base-monetisation leg of the thesis; **no estimate or guidance impact quantified by the note, and none inferred here.** |

Nothing superseded.** **Starship Flight 13 (Friday 2026-07-24) was operationally successful. MS (Adam Jonas) grades it 'A-' and says it MATERIALLY IMPROVED CONFIDENCE IN STARSHIP'S PATH TOWARD OPERATIONAL SERVICE, addressing near-term concerns after the JULY 17 LAUNCH ABORT — which is the specific overhang this flight needed to clear.** Milestones: **all 33 Super Heavy engines clean on ascent; successful hot-stage separation; FIRST DEPLOYMENT OF 20 PRODUCTION STARLINK V3 SATELLITES; successful in-space Raptor relight; and the BEST REENTRY AND SPLASHDOWN TO DATE with the ship intact and transmitting telemetry** (Bernstein notes the splashdown itself was hard — both characterisations kept). **The successor catalyst is now dated and is materially bigger than Flight 13 was: Musk indicated FLIGHT 14 MAY ATTEMPT THE FIRST-EVER CATCH OF A STARSHIP UPPER STAGE ON THE MECHAZILLA TOWER ARMS, expected LATE AUGUST OR EARLY SEPTEMBER 2026 — and a ship catch would likely require Starship's first ORBITAL trajectory, potentially enabling the first deployment of OPERATIONAL Starlink V3 satellites. Bernstein puts FULL REUSE IN SIGHT AT FLIGHT 15.** **The investment argument both houses make is the same and it is about what is not yet in the price: 'full reuse is key for SpaceX to set launch rates above any competitor, though THE MARKET HAS YET TO UNDERWRITE THESE LAUNCHES' — with the LOCKUP OVERHANG BEGINNING TO DISSIPATE FOLLOWING 2Q/3Q EPS ON 8/4.** Jonas adds that the pullback created an attractive entry since fundamentals are largely unchanged while sentiment turned increasingly bearish. **Two competitive items logged alongside: [[AMZN]] applied to the FCC for 5,105 SATELLITES for a direct-to-device service (post-Globalstar, alongside its Leo broadband build) — The Information's caution being that 'it won't be as profitable a business for either SpaceX or Amazon once they're both in it'; and Bernstein's US telco work concluding that while Starlink has substantial broadband runway, MOBILE DISRUPTION MAY NOT BE AS IMMINENT AS THE MARKET PRESUMED — an MVNO looks unlikely near-term and incumbents largely view satellite as a replacement to terrestrial, so the risk 'will linger into 2027 and 2028' rather than landing in 2H26.** Also: **Exploration Company GmbH, a SPCX rival, is in talks to raise $300M at a $2B valuation**; and SPCX joined the NVDA/[[MSFT]] AI-safety initiative. Cross-filed [[AMZN]], [[NVDA]], [[MSFT]]. |

| 2026-07-27 | ⭐ SMIC | **/run-inbox — The Information (Staff), "China Starts Mass-Producing Homegrown DUV Chipmaking Tools, An Advance for Local Chip Industry" (2026-07-27). Additive; no rating, PT, estimate, guidance or management figure superseded.** Ingested and labelled throughout as **single-outlet, anonymously-sourced ("two people familiar") news reporting — not a company disclosure, not a broker note, no rating/PT/estimate**; SMIC did not respond to a comment request. **Net-new to this page:** (1) **SMIC is a named 2026 delivery recipient** (with Hua Hong Semiconductor and CXMT) of the **first domestically mass-produced immersion DUV lithography machines**, built by a **Shanghai-based state-backed company the article deliberately leaves unnamed**, which pulled immersion-DUV development teams from other Chinese firms **including the state-backed equipment startup Shanghai Yuliangsheng Technology**; prototypes went to some chipmakers last year and encouraging test results triggered production this year. (2) **The sizing that keeps it honest — ~5 machines in 2026 and ~20 in 2027 against the 131 immersion DUV systems [[ASML]] shipped last year** — so this is a strategic-direction datapoint, not a 2026-27 capacity datapoint. (3) Four gates, all quoted rather than paraphrased: the tools **"still trail ASML's products in performance and build quality"**; mass-production insertion **"could take many months or longer"** as chipmakers test accuracy, reliability and tool-compatibility; **mostly domestic content but some key parts still come from Japan**, with **local-supplier delays holding back this year's output**; and China's **domestic EUV remains prototype-stage and "likely years away"** from working chips — i.e. **the EUV ceiling on SMIC's roadmap is unchanged**. (4) **First application is mature-node capacity, including power-management components essential to AI servers/data centers** — it maps to the 12-inch legacy base and the AI-power-management pull management flagged in Q1'26, not to N+3. (5) **Independent corroboration of the multiple-patterning workaround** this page documents from the SemiAnalysis/STEEL teardown: "SMIC has used that approach to produce advanced chips for smartphones and AI systems despite lacking access to EUV." (6) **A new, sharper policy exposure: the Match Act would curb SERVICING of tools at some Chinese fabs in addition to widening immersion-DUV sales restrictions** — the page previously carried only the sales/service *ban* framing from the AlphaSense ASML primer (2026-06-22); servicing degrades the **existing** ASML fleet's uptime and yield, and ~5-20 domestic units/yr cannot backfill it. (7) **A demand item from SMIC's own management that this page did not carry: CEO Zhao Haijun said on the May earnings call that overseas customers were looking to make chips in China because capacity was tight elsewhere** — a non-domestic demand leg against an ~85-89% domestic revenue base (⚠️ second-hand — The Information's characterization of the call, not a transcript line captured here). (8) Structural backdrop: lithography a Chinese national priority since **2002**; the **2022 US controls pushed Beijing from state-led research toward commercial execution with Huawei coordinating industry resources**. **Edits: 1 bullet under Advanced-node / Huawei supply line, 1 bullet under Current state (the Zhao Haijun demand point), 1 Catalysts bullet (domestic-DUV qualification watch), 1 Risks bullet (tool-servicing risk as distinct from tool-purchase risk), 1 Intra-quarter Full-log row (07-27, supply/mixed), a new "Tooling / supply" row in the Signal-vs-management table plus an append to the Demand/2H-risk row, an append to the Q1'26 "AI / memory-driven tightness" cell of the Management-commentary table, a Quarter-synthesis append, and 1 Sources link.** Deliberately NOT added: any claim that the domestic tool relieves SMIC's advanced-node constraint (the article says the opposite on EUV and gives a mature-node first use case), and no restatement of the Match Act's SMIC naming, which the page already carries from the AlphaSense primer. Cross-read [[ASML]] (folded there this run as a bear/structural-risk datapoint sized against the 131-unit base rate). |

Second, and cutting the other way, independent corroboration arrived the SAME DAY: Meta's Chief AI Officer Alexandr Wang told YC Startup School that Meta will build a HARNESS for its models plus open-source LLMs — precisely the agentic framework an SMB storefront product requires — and that 'the best AI products are yet to come'.** So the capability build is real and dated even if the commerce product is not yet announced. **The number that decides it is the 50x P/E: at that multiple the stock is priced for uninterrupted SMB share, and the debate is no longer about Shopify's execution but about whether its largest traffic source becomes its competitor.** Also noted: SHOP appeared on CNBC's biggest-analyst-calls list 07-22. Cross-filed [[META]], [[GOOG]]. |

Only net-new was added.** **The single best addition is an execution fact, not an opinion: UBS's ~90% EMIB-T packaging-yield figure is measured "based on trial production runs of MediaTek's TPU v9 prototype" — so MediaTek's v9 silicon has already been through EMIB-T trial production. That is meaningfully further along than "design intent," and it is the first source on this page to attach the packaging-yield number to MediaTek's own part.** **Second, the full note reconciles the yield spread this page (and [[INTC]]) has been treating as contradictory: UBS gives FOUR distinct figures — standard-EMIB PACKAGING >90%, standard-EMIB SUBSTRATE ~60%, EMIB-T SUBSTRATE ~50% (leading Japan suppliers), EMIB-T PACKAGING ~90% (the MediaTek prototype runs). Fubon's flat "60-70%" and UBS's "~50%" are substrate marks; Google's 95%+ gate and the 98-99% marks are packaging marks. They were never measuring the same thing.** What stays genuinely open is the bar, stated by UBS as **"for HPC products… packaging yields ideally should improve to the high 90% range"** — ~90% today vs a high-90s requirement, a narrower gap than "not MP-ready" implies but not closed. **Third, a new supply dependency two steps removed from MediaTek's control and worth raising on the 7/31 call: Ibiden's ¥220.0bn Gama-plant investment (Feb-2026) puts EMIB-T MP at late 2027 with a meaningful 2028 ramp, but UBS's checks say "demand from Google TPU programs alone could largely absorb the planned capacity"** — so v9 volume beyond the first tranche hangs on **Ibiden's next capex decision**, not on Intel's yield. UBS also has Intel prioritizing **internal products plus Google TPU v9 "which is being developed by MediaTek and set for mass production in 2028"** — MediaTek is first in the external queue, which means priority access *and* absorbing the ramp risk. **Fourth, UBS's Google-TPU EMIB-T volume path, logged with an explicit unit-basis warning: 2.3mn packages (2028E) / 3.5mn (2029E) / 5.0mn (2030E) base case, 2.3 / 4.5 / 6.5mn upside, at US$2,175 / 2,393 / 2,632 of EMIB-T content per package. standard-EMIB SUBSTRATE "close to 60%"; EMIB-T SUBSTRATE "around 50% for the leading Japan suppliers"; EMIB-T PACKAGING "about 90%, based on trial production runs of MediaTek's TPU v9 prototype."** So Fubon's 60-70% and UBS's ~50% are substrate marks while KeyBanc's 98% and ex-Intel-CVP Kaizad's 98-99% are packaging marks — they were never measuring the same thing. **The 07-26 framing that this page carries "three incompatible readings" is therefore partly retired; **EMIB-T substrates are "5x or even larger" than today's, potentially >9-10x reticle at 20+ layers**, requiring **TC NCF rather than standard die attach** into panel-level (510×515mm²) cavities; but the **equipment set is SIMPLER than CoPoS's** (no chip-on-panel stage), making **TCB tool demand (K&S, ASMPT) the named leading indicator of EMIB-T capacity expansion**, with Intel likely leaning on **US-based** equipment suppliers; the **assembly footprint is Penang (expanding, vertically integrated for external customers) plus Amkor's Korea/Portugal/US lines under the 2025 partnership**, with Intel expected to keep Google TPUs in-house and outsource mature CPU on-substrate work (**Amkor Neutral, PT US$80**); and UBS's **head-to-head scorecard — EMIB-T wins architecture simplicity, package complexity, scalability >12x reticle, cost (assuming reasonable yield) and time-to-market; CoPoS wins performance, power integrity, yield confidence and ecosystem; manufacturing risk a TIE** — plus a rarely-cited Intel edge, its **SMT expertise for board-level warpage management**. Rating rationale restated because it matters for how the page reads UBS: **Neutral is a valuation call ("we remain Neutral due to valuation considerations"), not a verdict on the EMIB-T ramp.** Cross-filed [[TSM]], [[MEDIATEK]], [[AVGO]], [[TOKYOELEC]]. |

| 2026-07-26 | ⭐ TSM | **4 intra-quarter rows. No rating, PT or estimate superseded** (Fubon Buy NT$3,500, UBS Buy, Nomura Buy, BofA all stand). **The headline is that the EMIB-T threat finally got stress-tested by a house that covers both sides, and TSMC wins in the bull case for Intel.** UBS sizes the **advanced-packaging TAM at US$19bn (2026E) → US$122bn (2030E), a 59% CAGR**, and then hands Intel its best plausible outcome — **13% industry share / US$16bn of sales by 2030, packages for ~24mn accelerator dies, with CoPoS not reaching MP for Nvidia until 2028 and for others until 2029-30** — and TSMC *still* compounds advanced-packaging sales at a **50% CAGR 2026-30, holds 65-70% share, and takes advanced packaging to 18% of total sales in 2030E from 9% in 2026E.** In UBS's *lower* case (stronger EMIB-T adoption) that becomes **47% CAGR / 62% share / 16% of sales** — i.e. **the base-vs-bear spread is roughly 2pts of revenue mix over four years.** That is the most useful bound on packaging-disruption risk this page has, and it argues the debate is worth far less to TSMC's numbers than to Intel's. **Not smoothed over: UBS also says EMIB-T reaches MP in H2'27-28, slightly AHEAD of CoPoS in 2028, and names TSMC's own three execution risks — "CoPoS warpage, panel process uniformity, and new equipment readiness."** The compensating technical case: CoPoS offers "tighter interconnect, better signal integrity, broader integration options such as 3D stacking (and eventually, CPO)"; EMIB-T's edge is structural cost (no interposer) and scalability to ultra-large packages on large substrates. UBS's own framing of the outcome — **EMIB-T as "a meaningful second-source for selected hyperscaler ASICs" while CoPoS stays "the preferred platform for customers prioritizing the highest performance and execution reliability"** — is a share-of-mind concession, not a share-of-revenue one. **Second, and it corroborates the above from a completely different seat: an ex-ASM International senior process engineer (AlphaSense expert call, 07-25) independently concludes that TSMC's dominance holds and that the binding constraint is TOOL availability — specifically bonding tools — "not… financial or market factors,"** with demand for CoWoS and CoPoS "outpacing supply for the foreseeable future," the market **bifurcated (TSMC in complex, OSATs in mature)**, and the TSMC-NVIDIA relationship "deeply integrated… making it difficult for competitors to break in." Caveat kept on the page: it is a 2-page expert-network summary and the expert's background is front-end ALD/process integration, so it is a well-informed prior rather than a supply-chain audit. **Third — a pattern worth naming rather than a one-off: on glass-core substrates TSMC is ALSO behind Intel.** MS's checks have **Innolux + TSMC + Ibiden reaching MP only in 2H28 or 2029, versus Intel/Lens Tech with a pilot line by end-2026 and meaningful volume from 2H27** — the same ~12-18-month ordering as EMIB-T vs CoPoS. On both bridges and substrates, Intel is earlier and TSMC is betting on the higher-performance version arriving later. Glass core is directly relevant because it attacks **warpage**, one of the three CoPoS risks UBS names. **Fourth, restated because it is management's own words and was not on this page in this form: "TSMC highlighted at its 2Q26 call that back-end sits in deeper shortage than front-end, with packaging capacity so tight it now limits customer growth"** (via Jefferies) — i.e. packaging, not wafers, is the named growth constraint, which is the demand-side justification for the entire capex build above, plus **two ADDITIONAL advanced-packaging fabs announced on the call** (a positive read to [[KLAC]]'s Advanced Packaging SPC line, C26 forecast $1B / +57% y/y). Minor housekeeping note: BofA's weekend desk recap carried TSMC 2026 capex as "$58B" in a one-line summary while its own semicap section used **$60-64B from $52-56B** — the **$60-64B guide already confirmed on this page (07-16 call) stands**; the $58B is a modelled/actual figure, not the guide. Cross-filed [[INTC]], [[NVDA]], [[KLAC]], [[MEDIATEK]], [themes/cowos-packaging](themes/cowos-packaging.md), [themes/optical-cpo](themes/optical-cpo.md). |

CXMT **listed in Shanghai 7/27** at a debut valuation BofA's desk framed as **15-20x FY27 P/E → ~RMB5T (bull 30x → ~RMB9T)** — a multiple of SK hynix's own market cap on roughly 10% share, i.e. **the threat is cost of capital, not technology.** The gate is export control (**MATCH Act**; CXMT equipment localisation ~40%; worst case its **Shanghai capacity — 30-40% of total by 2028F — pushed out**). **(2) The same note contains the strongest structural demand statement now on this page, and it also comes from the competitor's analyst: global memory usage up more than SEVENFOLD over 2026-30F (60%+ bit CAGR) "even after accounting for potential adoption of memory-efficiency technologies (discounting memory usage by 4x)," against industry bit expansion of only 30-40% CAGR — "that could still be insufficient."** A 4x efficiency haircut and demand still outruns supply. **(3) Positioning is genuinely contradictory across desks and is left that way, because the disagreement is the information: MS Prime has HFs recovering ~50% of the capital unwound through June with the Global Memory (MSXXGMEM) L/S ratio at ~7x vs peaks >20x — i.e. re-engaged but nowhere near crowded — and MS's EU client poll has 61% saying now is the time to add to memory longs (Semis the preferred 2H26 TMT sector at 57%). | 2026-07-26 | ⭐ SAMSUNG | (/wiki-ingest — Outlook, weekend batch into the 7/29 print). 5 intra-quarter rows. No rating, PT or estimate superseded** — but the page now carries what is, on its face, **the most consequential single datapoint of the quarter: Samsung announced a $200bn / 5-year chip supply contract with [[AVGO]] covering memory, foundry AND advanced packaging** (Reuters, 2026-07-25: "Samsung Elec wins $200 billion Broadcom AI chip partnership, boosting foundry push"). It matters for three reasons, in order: **(1) it is a foundry win**, and the standing bear on this page has been precisely that Samsung Foundry cannot land a marquee leading-edge logic customer at scale — Broadcom is the largest merchant custom-ASIC house there is; **(2) it bundles DRAM/HBM + foundry + advanced packaging into one counterparty**, which is the "turnkey" pitch Samsung has been making against the TSMC-plus-separate-OSAT model, now with a reference customer; **(3) ~$40bn/yr against Samsung's current foundry run-rate is transformational and, if it converts, forces the capex line materially higher** — BofA's desk drew that inference the same day ("bodes well for Samsung capex"), which makes it a WFE read-through as well ([[KLAC]] / [[LRCX]] / [[AMAT]] / [[ASML]], filed to [themes/semicap-wfe](themes/semicap-wfe.md)). **Deliberately NOT treated as a booked number:** as reported it is a supply-contract/partnership *value*, not backlog; no node, product or phasing split has been disclosed; and BofA's own weekend note hedged it as "AVGO **possibly** doing $200B deal with Samsung foundry," i.e. the desk was reading the same press we are. **Management gets its first chance to size or qualify it on 7/29 — that is the thing to watch, not the headline.** One cross-read worth holding: Hock Tan told JPM on 07-07 that Broadcom keeps majority TPU volume with Google, so this plausibly attaches to non-Google ASIC programs and/or HBM/packaging supply rather than implying a Google-share shift. **Second thread — the AMD content position is now explicitly two-sided and both sides stay on the page:** Jefferies reports Samsung "currently supplies HBM4 for AMD's MI455 Instinct GPUs and serves as the dedicated supplier of DDR5 solutions for the 6th-generation EPYC CPUs deployed in the Helios platform," while SemiAnalysis (07-21) documented a quantified HBM-content cut on a Meta-specific cut-down MI part and (07-24) that MSFT "dropped AMD after MI300X due to unreliable Samsung 2023 HBM memory." Named flagship supplier and carrier of the quality stigma on the same account — the **dedicated DDR5 sole-source language on 6th-gen EPYC is the cleaner win**. **Third: an [[AAPL]] System-LSI content gain — Samsung (with Novatek) takes iPhone 18 display-driver-IC share from LX Semicon, whose iPhone DDI share goes from 50%± (2022-25) to ~10%± on iPhone 18 (so ~90%+ to Novatek/Samsung), on Pro / Pro Max / foldable**, and for a technical reason (iPhone 18 "requires more advanced power/heating control") rather than price — which makes it more durable (BofA/Simon Woo, 07-26; LXS downgraded to Underperform, PO W63,000 → W25,000). Also logged: **South Korea's ~$950bn of new AI initiatives** announced around President Lee's US tour (Reuters, 07-25), and BofA reiterating both its **memory-bull stance into "a big week for memory — CXMT IPO, Kioxia, Samsung"** and its expectation of a **massive buyback + earlier dividends at the 7/29 call** (already on page from 07-24). | 2026-07-26 | OPENAI | **4 intra-quarter rows. Private co — nothing superseded, and deliberately NOT added to the compute-obligations table.** **The item of the weekend: WSJ reports Nvidia is in talks to provide a $250bn backstop for OpenAI, to help it lease a 10GW Ohio data-center project from SoftBank, with the project costing ~$500bn including chips.** Structurally this is different from everything already in the obligations table (**Oracle ~$300bn, Azure ~$250bn, ~$650bn total**): in this one **OpenAI is not the party carrying the balance-sheet risk — SoftBank owns the asset, NVDA guarantees the lease, OpenAI takes the capacity, NVDA books the chip revenue.** It is the GPU-debt-backstop mechanism from SemiAnalysis's "AI Project Trinity" (07-06, filed on [[NVDA]]) scaled to a single site and a single tenant, at roughly an order of magnitude beyond anything previously disclosed. **Two consequences: (1) if it converts, it materially reduces the equity and credit OpenAI itself must raise to secure 10GW — the tightest constraint in the buildout as modelled on this page; (2) it sharpens the circular-financing critique, because NVDA would be credit-enhancing its own customer's landlord.** JPM's read is a market test, not a catalyst — "if another $250bn guarantee isn't enough, then well… you really have to wonder what is" — with the SOX already -19% off its June high. **Status: talks per WSJ; unconfirmed by OpenAI, NVDA or SoftBank; the $250bn is NOT carried as a commitment and the obligations table is unchanged pending disclosure.** **Second: Altman travels to Washington this week to preview OpenAI's most powerful models** (Axios) — in the same week the administration is deciding how to treat Chinese open-weight models, which is unlikely to be coincidental. **Third, and logged with the tension intact: NYT reports OpenAI and [[ANTHROPIC]] are lobbying the White House to restrict Chinese open-weight models "despite what they may say," being "increasingly 'rattled' by China's advances," with the administration "leaning towards regulating Chinese models individually as a national security issue instead of issuing a blanket ban" — while NVDA published an open letter (MSFT co-leading) urging Washington NOT to impose sweeping restrictions.** The split is now cleanly along the stack: compute wants open weights free, the model layer wants them constrained. Per-model national-security review looks like the landing zone. **Fourth, pricing and — more usefully — same-benchmark cost: gpt-5.6-sol at ~$5.00/$30.00 per M in/out (≤272K input), between Fable 5 ($10/$50) and Grok 4.5 ($2/$6); but on total cost to run the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index the frontier tier (Fable 5 $5,631 / Opus 4.8 $3,753) sits far above open weights (Kimi-K3 $2,710, qwen3.7-plus $311, deepseek-v4-flash $74).** JPM's structural finding is the important one for pricing power: on Epoch AI's ECI the top-3 open vs top-3 closed gap has been **"relatively stable… rather than materially converging"** — the open models are cheaper, not catching up. OpenAI sits inside the routing layers designed to send simple work elsewhere (**MSFT Foundry Model Router, Intuit GenOS**), and WSJ (07-26) reports CTOs "reserving only the most specialized and complex queries for OpenAI and Anthropic." Dedup: the ~$750bn through-2030 compute-spend figure and the Hugging Face containment item were both filed 07-24/07-25 and were not re-added. Cross-filed [[NVDA]], [[ANTHROPIC]], [[MSFT]], [[ORCL]], [themes/tokenmaxxing](themes/tokenmaxxing.md). |

| 2026-07-26 | ⭐ NVDA | (/wiki-ingest — Outlook, weekend batch). 6 intra-quarter rows. No rating, PT or estimate superseded** (MS Top Pick $288, BofA $320/$350, Barclays OW $275, Cantor all stand). **Two items dominate and they point in opposite directions, which is the honest state of the name.** **(1) THE BACKSTOP GOES TO $250BN — reported, not announced. WSJ says Nvidia is in talks to provide a $250bn backstop for [[OPENAI]] to help it lease a 10GW Ohio data-center project from SoftBank, with the project costing ~$500bn including the chips.** This is the **exact mechanism already documented on this page from SemiAnalysis's "AI Project Trinity" (07-06), scaled to one counterparty and one site, at roughly an order of magnitude above anything previously disclosed** — NVDA guarantees the lease, SoftBank owns the asset, OpenAI takes the capacity, NVDA books the chips. **Status is talks per WSJ; not confirmed by NVDA, OpenAI or SoftBank, and NOT carried here as a commitment.** JPM's framing is the one worth keeping, because it inverts the usual read: this is a *test* of the tape, not a catalyst — "the sum of money in discussion is gargantuan… **provides yet another test of whether 'good news' around capex can lift semi stocks. If another $250bn guarantee isn't enough, then well… you really have to wonder what is**" — with the SOX already **-4.3% Friday and -19% off the mid-June high**. It will, in JPM's words, "invariably raise circular financing debates again." **(2) THE MEMORY BILL IS NOW VISIBLY CUTTING NVDA'S OWN SPECS, and this is the clearest margin-transfer mechanism yet logged on this page.** Jefferies' O'Mara, citing WCCFTECH: after the reported **RTX 50 ramp delay on memory costs** and the **SOCAMM spec cut**, NVDA is **now also cutting the memory spec on NVL72** — because **"memory is the majority of the BOM on VR200."** The dispersion in sell-side inputs is the striking part: rack-cost models are running on **HBM $/GB assumptions from ~$20 (Jefferies' own Sherman, which O'Mara says is too low) to $53/GB in 2027 — a ~2.7x spread** — with one broker at **~$9.1m per rack**. O'Mara's conclusion: "Nvidia has a memory problem in its BOM and earnings structure… trying to address this by reducing the specs across multiple products." Trade-press + desk opinion, not NVDA disclosure — but it is the first *observable* evidence that the memory upcycle this book is long elsewhere ([[MU]], [[SKHYNIX]], [[SNDK]]) is being paid for partly out of NVDA's bill of materials. **(3) A clean policy split now sits between the compute layer and the model layer: NVDA published an open letter urging Washington NOT to impose sweeping restrictions on open-weight models (with MSFT co-leading, post-Kimi K3), while [[OPENAI]] and [[ANTHROPIC]] are reported to be lobbying FOR restrictions** ("increasingly rattled by China's advances"; the administration leaning toward per-model national-security review rather than a blanket ban — NYT). Consistent with UBS's read that at the GPU layer open-model traction reallocates rather than reduces demand and is **"bullish Nvidia."** **(4) Two expert calls with hard engineering numbers: next-gen GPUs reaching up to 2,400W TDP with D2C liquid cooling the current standard and advanced alternatives (two-phase immersion, TEC, new TIMs) "two to three years away" from commercial deployment; and the cost governor that cooling "should not exceed 9-10% of total data center operating or CapEx costs."** Separately, an MSFT data-center program manager puts **solid-state transformers as the eventual 800V DC standard but "not… widespread adoption before 2030-2032"** — ⚠️ **materially later than the vendor timeline on [[GEV]]'s page** (5MW indoor SST shipping to a hyperscaler this year, 6MW outdoor over 18 months, revenue "'27 and beyond"). Logged as a vendor-vs-buyer gap, not resolved. **(5) New non-tech verticals: BMY deploying Vera Rubin racks and a 5-year LLY/NVDA agreement** — pharma is new to this page — plus real-world token evidence from **AT&T at ~1tn+ tokens/month / ~16.4tn annualized, >100 GenAI models in production (~40% on AMD hardware)**. Also: **Feynman GPU + Rosa CPU targeting TSMC A16 around 2028** (Commercial Times, trade press). BofA's **NVDA buy-side bogey poll is open into the 8/26 AMC print**. Cross-filed [[OPENAI]], [[MU]], [[TSM]], [[AMD]], [[GEV]], [themes/hbm-memory](themes/hbm-memory.md), [themes/800v-dc-power](themes/800v-dc-power.md). |
| 2026-07-26 | ⭐ MU | (/wiki-ingest — Outlook, weekend batch). 4 intra-quarter rows. No rating, PT or estimate superseded** (BofA Buy $1,550, TD Cowen Buy $1,600, Mizuho, Susquehanna, MS all stand). **The competitive side of this page changed materially this weekend, and it did so through a bull note on the competitor rather than a bear note on MU. Nomura initiated coverage of ChangXin Memory (CXMT, 688825 CH) at Buy, TP CNY116 — and benchmarked the multiple explicitly off Micron: CXMT should trade at "2x the valuation of its major US competitor, Micron… which has traded at ~10x over the past five years."** The forecast that matters here: **CXMT's global DRAM share going from ~10% today to ~18% by end-2028F on 40-45% bit-CAGR expansion 2026-30F.** That **independently corroborates Susquehanna's 17%-of-DRAM-supply-by-YE28 estimate already on this page (07-25)** — and it does so from a house that is long the stock, which makes the agreement more informative than two bears converging. **CXMT listed in Shanghai on 7/27**, with BofA's APAC desk reporting a **base-case valuation of 15-20x FY27 P/E → ~RMB5T market cap and a bull case of 30x → ~RMB9T** (roughly US$700bn-1.3tn on ~10% DRAM share) — the cleanest illustration on this page of the **cost-of-capital asymmetry MU faces against a state-supported competitor**, and the reason the ASP threat is not simply a function of CXMT's technology. **Both sides of the CXMT question are kept, because they are different arguments and both are true: it is a commodity-DRAM ASP threat, and per BofA/Arya it "remains largely confined to commodity DRAM and is not a credible near-term competitor in HBM3E/HBM4 AI memory."** **Second: Micron is now publicly on the record against an Apple lobbying push — WSJ reports Apple lobbying the White House "aggressively for permission to utilize Chinese memory chips in devices sold outside the US" with MU "fiercely opposed," warning China's makers "could decimate the American memory industry over time just as occurred in steel and manufacturing."** Escalations vs the 06-25 story on file: the ask is now a specific and much more grantable **ex-US carve-out**, and MU's opposition carries an explicit industrial-policy frame. The 06-29 KIS rebuttal still stands as the counter (China restricts foreign supply anyway; CXMT shifted LPDDR→DDR for domestic DCs) — so this changes no supply yet, but it does establish that customer pressure has reached the White House, in the same week as the CXMT initiation and IPO. **Third, the bullish half of Nomura's own work is the strongest demand statement now on this page and it comes from the competitor's analyst: global memory usage rising more than SEVENFOLD over 2026-30F (60%+ bit CAGR) "even after accounting for potential adoption of memory-efficiency technologies (discounting memory usage by 4x)," against all memory makers expanding at only a 30-40% bit CAGR — "that could still be insufficient."** A 4x efficiency haircut still leaves demand outrunning supply by a wide margin; that is the tightest formulation of the structural bull case on file. **Fourth, restated with the consensus gap explicit: BofA/Simon Woo's July checks have Tier-2 OEMs and module makers paying 15-20% higher PC DRAM contract prices m/m, implying +30-40% q/q against TrendForce's +13-18% for 3Q ex-HBM** — and the tape is paying for it, with **MU +8% last week (STX +8%, WDC +9%, SNDK +6%) while the SOX sat -19% off its high.** Also net-new from Arya's defence: **Kimi K3 requires ~1.4TB of HBM per serving instance despite ultra-low API pricing**, and open-weight models "multiply memory demand because every customer deployment creates a new memory endpoint" — the Jevons argument stated as architecture rather than elasticity. ⚠️ One data-integrity note: Nomura's quoted **closing price of CNY8.66 against a CNY116 TP** cannot be reconciled with a 20x-2028F-EPS-of-CNY5.8 derivation; the stock listed 7/27, so the close is pre-listing/stale and only the TP is usable. Cross-filed [[AAPL]], [[SAMSUNG]], [[SKHYNIX]], [[SNDK]], [themes/hbm-memory](themes/hbm-memory.md), [themes/china-export](themes/china-export.md). |

Nothing superseded** (UBS Neutral $240, DB Hold $220, MS $274, GS $230, TD Cowen Buy $260, Bernstein OP $197.50 all stand). **Two things are net-new and one is a live disagreement worth carrying into the call.** **(1) Jefferies' preview is the most specific on the page: beat & raise with revenue growth guided to "10%+"/double-digit vs Consensus at 9%, and the framing explains the stock — "KLAC YTD performance (+78%) has lagged large-cap peers with management offering a less optimistic near-term forecast of high-teens WFE growth to $140B in C26."** So the stock is discounting management's own conservatism, and the catch-up case is external: **INTC accelerating process-control investment plus KLAC's own supply constraints easing in 2H.** The two hard bogeys for the print: **Advanced Packaging SPC C26 revenue raised to $1B on the April call, +57% y/y** (with TSMC's **two additional advanced-packaging fabs** announced on its 2Q26 call a further positive read), and **a 100bps gross-margin headwind from elevated DRAM costs running through C26, with a September GM bogey of 62% in line with Street.** Jefferies is explicit that it **still prefers AMAT** — this is a 2H catch-up trade, not a preference switch. **(2) The disagreement to watch is on pricing, and it is direct: BofA/Vivek Arya says "pricing is mostly an untapped gross margin expansion source" while Jefferies says "we expect commentary from LRCX/AMAT/KLAC management to be more tempered."** That matters more for KLAC than for the others because the DB/UBS pricing hierarchy already on this page ranks it **last (ASML > AMAT > LRCX > KLAC), with KLAC named "the least likely in the group to pursue pricing"** — so BofA's group-wide pricing optimism is hardest to apply here. The call is the place to resolve it. Also logged: **BofA's WFE track $144B/$190B/$250B for CY26-28 (its C26 figure sits ABOVE management's ~$140B guide)**, and the customer-capex evidence that accumulated in one week — **TSM 2026 capex to $60-64B from $52-56B, INTC to $20B+ with 2027 "significantly" higher, and the reported $200bn AVGO-Samsung deal reading through to Samsung capex.** Positioning is contradictory and left that way: BofA's charts have **LOs far less overweight semicaps than in early 2023** with the inflection only starting mid-2025, JPM has **global semis at the 98th %ile on net exposure and L/S ratios** on long-term metrics, and Jefferies' own desk has **net sold semicaps across HFs and LOs for four straight weeks** into a **-18% sub-sector derating (-20% P/E) since the June 22 SOX peak.** Cross-filed [[LRCX]], [[TSM]], [[INTC]], [[SAMSUNG]], [themes/semicap-wfe](themes/semicap-wfe.md). |
| 2026-07-26 | ⭐ INTC | **/wiki-ingest (Outlook, weekend batch). 4 intra-quarter rows. No rating, PT or estimate superseded** (UBS Neutral $121, MS EW $84, JPM UW $85, Barclays EW $100, BofA Buy $160, Arete Neutral $99 all stand). **The material item is UBS's first full-length EMIB-T-vs-CoPoS deep dive, and its contribution is to make the yield picture on this page WORSE-defined, not better — which is the honest outcome and is recorded as such.** UBS says Intel "must lift **EMIB-T packaging from ~90-92% validation yields** toward high-volume manufacturing levels," and that **EMIB-T substrate fabrication yields "may currently be at ~50%, vs >80% for HPC ABF substrates."** Set that against the marks already on the page — **Fubon 60-70% (06-05), KeyBanc "reached 98%" (07-24), ex-Intel CVP Kaizad "mature yield 98-99%" (07-16)** — and the **packaging-yield estimates now span 60% to 99%, while substrate estimates span ~50% (UBS) to 60-70% (KeyBanc)**. Two things make this tractable rather than noise: UBS's figure is the **only one explicitly labelled *validation* yield** (which may reconcile part of the spread against production-yield marks), and UBS's own base case **routes around the substrate problem rather than solving it — "capacity expansion at Ibiden could offset lower yields and support selected products like Google's TPU v9."** So the constraint identified on 07-25 (substrates, not bridges) survives, with a lower number attached and a named workaround. **Not resolved. The 95%+ Google TPU-V9 gate remains the single item on which the Foundry thesis turns, and this page now carries three incompatible readings of it.** Net-new sizing: **advanced-packaging TAM US$19bn (2026E) → US$122bn (2030E), a 59% CAGR**, with UBS's *bullish* Intel stress test at **13% industry share / US$16bn of sales by 2030 (~24mn accelerator dies)** — a case in which [[TSM]] still compounds advanced-packaging sales at 50% and holds 65-70% share. **EMIB-T's structural advantages, per UBS: interposer elimination (structural cost), and scalability to ultra-large packages by building directly on large substrates — with MP targeted H2'27-28, slightly AHEAD of CoPoS in 2028.** And the two Intel-specific risks UBS names that were not on this page: **"integration between the foundry (TSMC) and back-end (Intel) processes, as well as yield responsibility"** — a contracting problem (who eats a yield loss when TSMC owns the die and Intel owns the package), not a technical one, and arguably the harder of the two to fix. Rating stays Neutral **explicitly on valuation**, not on execution. **Second net-new thread — glass core substrates now have a dated Intel ramp: Lens Tech (300433.SZ) is partnering with Intel on glass core with TGVs, pilot line ready by end-2026 and "more meaningful volume shipments might start from 2H27"** (MS/Derrick Yang), possibly with Korean players too. That is the structural answer to the substrate-yield constraint (lower signal loss, CTE matching against warpage, mechanical strength at panel scale), and on MS's checks Intel is **~12-18 months ahead of the TSMC/Ibiden glass path (Innolux MP 2H28-2029; BOE capacity 2027 for 2028 MP)** — the same ordering UBS gives for EMIB-T vs CoPoS. MS's caveat kept: helps sentiment, "more meaningful revenue contribution should take time," hurdles are "process control instead of pure equipment capability." **Third: 14A described as ahead of where 18A was — "defect density and transistor performance are outpacing 18A development"** (Jefferies), with Intel "aggressively locking in tool purchase orders and accelerating cleanroom buildouts," 14A "likely to be a material user of high-NA," and [[ASML]] revenue from Intel "subdued this year but likely to accelerate in 2027." The gate is unchanged and worth repeating: **the WFE step-up requires a named 14A customer, and official C27 guidance is ~6 months out.** **Fourth, the tape, recorded because it is the actual outcome: INTC closed -8% Friday having been +5% pre-market on results JPM's Harlan Sur said "cleared expectations by wide margins" — the SOX is -19% from its mid-June high, CPUs have derated -21% since June 22, and INTC is -34% since the Tech-Momentum ATH.** JPM's positioning work explains part of it — aggregate equity positioning light (US TPM 19th %ile) but **global semis crowded at the 98th %ile on both net exposure and L/S ratios**. Cross-filed [[TSM]], [[ASML]], [[MEDIATEK]], [[GOOG]], [themes/cowos-packaging](themes/cowos-packaging.md), [themes/semicap-wfe](themes/semicap-wfe.md). |
| 2026-07-26 | IFX | (/wiki-ingest — Outlook, weekend batch). 1 intra-quarter row. Nothing superseded.** 🔴 **The differentiator: Jefferies names Infineon as the ONE analog company where pricing becomes a revenue and margin driver — "price increases have, however, not yet become a driver of revenue and margin broadly in the sector, though we expect a stronger impact at IFX."** The pricing lever now visible in semicaps and at [[QCOM]] (double-digit hikes reported to customers, Bloomberg 07-24) is **not yet in analog numbers**, and Jefferies expects IFX to capture it first — which is the cleanest margin-upside argument on this page that does not depend on volume. **The backdrop is a confirmed broad upcycle: "trends at both STM and TI support our view that the analog segment has now entered a strong upcycle with rising orders in the data centre, industrial and automotive segments," with industrial revenue up over 30% y/y at BOTH TI and STM, data-centre revenue "rising sharply," TI seeing auto accelerating led by China, and "low inventories in the supply chain combined with signs of tightness… which we expect to continue well into 2027."** STM's H2-26 shortfall is explicitly ring-fenced as company-specific and none of it reads across: **a material y/y fall in Apple-related sales on the lack of Face ID in the foldable iPhone; a fab issue moving >$100m of revenue Q3→Q4; and a fab-conversion program costing ~400bps of gross margin.** Tape in IFX's favour: **analog derated only -14% since the June 22 SOX peak — the smallest of any sub-sector** (memory -24%, CPUs -21%, semicap -18%, optical -15%) — with Jefferies arguing analog is best placed to withstand the cyclical multiple compression because the earnings upgrades are arriving "combined with rising prices, which provides a cushion." Diary: **IFX previewed at BofA's EU Semis best-ideas call 7/27** (Scemama/Wong/Banks). Cross-filed [[NXPI]], [[TXN]], [[AAPL]]. |

Dedup: the UBS "buyers strike" / $15.50-16.00-at-20x / +$53m retail-inflow items in this weekend's UBS wrap were already filed 07-24 and were not re-added. | 2026-07-26 | ⭐ GLW | (/wiki-ingest — Outlook, weekend batch). 1 intra-quarter row. Nothing superseded.** **A new glass-intensive semiconductor end market is forming and the named participants are Asian display-chain incumbents, not Corning — logged as an open competitive question rather than an assumed win.** MS (Derrick Yang): **Lens Tech announced a partnership with Intel on glass core substrates with TGVs — pilot line ready by end-2026 and "more meaningful volume shipments might start from 2H27,"** possibly with Korean partners too; **Innolux + TSMC + Ibiden** at MP 2H28 or 2029; **BOE** with capacity in 2027 for 2028 MP, "aspiration to finish the whole glass core ABF substrate process"; **AUO** on glass for LEO antenna modules and micro-LED optical. The design-in rationale is squarely a materials argument — glass offers **"cost advantages from larger dimensions… better electrical performance, leading to lower signal loss, less mismatch of coefficients of thermal expansion… to mitigate the warpage issue, and greater mechanical strength"** — and **the value capture hinges on process control, not equipment: "the requirement for precision and density is much higher than what they used to deal with… if they can continue to improve the yield, we believe they will likely take a relatively big portion of the value added in the mass production stage."** The reason to track it here: **glass core is the structural answer to the ~50% ABF-substrate-yield constraint on Intel's EMIB-T and to CoPoS warpage at TSMC** (see [themes/cowos-packaging](themes/cowos-packaging.md)), so the TAM is real and growing with advanced packaging — the question is whether Corning is in it. GLW reports **7/28 BMO.** Cross-filed [[INTC]], [[TSM]]. |
| 2026-07-26 | ⭐ GEV | /wiki-ingest (Outlook, weekend batch). **4 intra-quarter rows. Nothing superseded** — but four call details are net-new and two of them directly address the fear the stock sold off on. **(1) Equipment pricing is now quantified: "1H26 orders were priced more than 20% above 4Q25 orders," with 2H gas equipment orders expected at a $/kW "at the higher end of the range of 10 to 20 points versus 4Q'25."** First hard price-realisation number on this page, against a dated base — the margin guide is being driven by price, not just volume. **(2) The 30 GW capacity target finally has its baseline, and it changes the read: it is a raise from a 24 GW FY28 plan, "through LEAN and add'l machinery."** The stock fell **-9%** on the print partly because a new ~30 GW capacity target revived overcapacity fears; a **+25% productivity raise off an existing 24 GW plan is a very different thing from a greenfield capital commitment**, and the page previously carried only the 30 GW endpoint. **(3) The forward book runs further than the page had it: "over half of '31 GWs will be on contract by the end of the year with active discussions for 2032 and beyond,"** and on peak-orders — **"continue to see very healthy growth above 125 gigawatts in '27 relative to year-end '26… confident in continuing to grow the contracted backlog for the better part of the next six quarters… even with the higher output."** **(4) Segment guidance for 2026: Power revenue +18-20% at 17-19% EBITDA margins; Electrification $14.5-15B revenue at 18-20% EBITDA margins; Wind revenue down LDD with ~$400mm of EBITDA losses.** Also logged: **~100 customers in 26 countries, 80% traditional / 20% data centers**, agreements into 2031, international pipeline (Taiwan, Saudi, Mexico, SE Asia, Qatar), and aeroderivatives explicitly framed as a **bridge** to heavy-duty rather than a substitute. **⚠️ One genuine cross-source gap logged rather than resolved — the SST adoption curve.** Management now describes **two distinct hyperscaler programs (a 5MW indoor unit shipping later this year for testing, and a 6MW outdoor unit with a different hyperscaler over ~18 months), both R&D-shared, with revenue "'27 and beyond."** But a **Microsoft data-centre program manager (AlphaSense expert call, 07-26, filed on [[NVDA]]) believes SST "will eventually become the industry standard for 800V DC" but "does not expect widespread adoption before 2030-2032,"** with 800V still "limited to pilot deployments" and most data centres on AC. GEV is selling into 2027; the buyer is planning for 2030-2032. Both can be true (pilots '27, standard '30-'32) but the implied revenue slopes differ a lot. Filed to [themes/800v-dc-power](themes/800v-dc-power.md). **Finally, a larger US supply-gap frame from BofA's oil-and-gas team: "US electricity demand requires the equivalent of 177GW of new capacity between 2026-30, but utilities are likely to supply just 93 gigawatts, leaving a gap exceeding 80GW,"** with **"hyperscalers plan[ning] to spend over $5trn in the next five years, yet only ~9% of US data centers have onsite generation"** and an 84GW opportunity if all behind-the-meter demand were met by gas. Note the source's own interest — the note is a Buy case for CVX/WMB/KGS/PUMP/LBRT as onsite-generation suppliers, i.e. partly substitution *away* from grid-scale CCGT — but the 177-vs-93GW gap is larger than the ">100GW shortfall" this page already carries from Obin's 07-08 "Power gap." Cross-filed [[NVDA]], [[CEG]], [[TLN]], [themes/ai-datacenter-power](themes/ai-datacenter-power.md), [themes/800v-dc-power](themes/800v-dc-power.md). |

| 2026-07-26 | ⭐ AVGO | **2 intra-quarter rows. Nothing superseded** (MS OW $502 / ~80% TPU share, and the Hock-Tan-07-07 majority-volume statement both stand — and are in fact reinforced). **Headline: Broadcom struck a $200bn / 5-year chip supply partnership with [[SAMSUNG]] spanning memory, foundry AND advanced packaging** (Reuters, 2026-07-25). From the AVGO side this reads as **supply security, not a customer or share event, and it lands squarely on the two constraints this page already tracks**: TSMC's own 2Q26 statement that **back-end is in deeper shortage than front-end with packaging capacity now limiting customer growth**, and HBM/memory allocation where NVDA's bidding power has been the standing worry in the ASIC-vs-GPU debate. Locking five years of Samsung memory + foundry + packaging is how a fabless XPU house de-risks a ramp it has already sold. **Explicitly not converted into a number here: it is a reported partnership value, not AVGO backlog; no node, product or phasing split is disclosed; and BofA's own desk hedged it as "AVGO *possibly* doing $200B deal with Samsung foundry."** Two open questions logged rather than answered — whether any **Google TPU volume migrates off TSMC** (Hock Tan's 07-07 "majority volume stays with Broadcom, MediaTek narrative is Taiwan PR" is about share, not fab), and how much of the $200bn is memory/packaging versus leading-edge logic. Samsung reports 7/29; AVGO's next print is where the split should appear. **Second row, and it sharpens the central bull/bear cleanly: MS (Charlie Chan) reconciles the firm's global TPU view to "MediaTek's 3nm TPU volume shipment may approach that of AVGO in 2027… however AVGO should remain the majority TPU supplier over time, with ~80% revenue share, given higher ASP on higher gross margin" — i.e. AVGO wins on ASP and mix, not on units.** The net-new sizing behind it is large and is MS's estimate rather than a disclosure: **a US$100bn TPU revenue TAM in 2027**, from which a 20% MediaTek share = US$20bn (above MediaTek's own US$7-12bn guide) and ~US$80bn is left to Broadcom. Directionally consistent with the Jefferies/Fubon 12-15M-TPU-chips-in-2028 figure already on the page. Also flagged as a new competitive vector: **Qualcomm entering ASIC design services**. Cross-filed [[SAMSUNG]], [[MEDIATEK]], [[TSM]], [[GOOG]], [themes/custom-asic-tpu](themes/custom-asic-tpu.md), [themes/cowos-packaging](themes/cowos-packaging.md). |
| 2026-07-26 | ⭐ ARM | (/wiki-ingest — Outlook, weekend batch). 1 intra-quarter row. Nothing superseded** (UBS PT $360 from 07-25 stands, as do BofA and MS marks). Context into the **7/29 AMC print**, which lands on one of the most crowded nights of the quarter ([[MSFT]], [[META]], [[LRCX]], [[QCOM]] all the same session). **Positioning is the setup: ARM is -36% since the Tech-Momentum ATH on June 22**, among the worst in Jefferies' universe (GFS -40%, NOK -37%, MRVL -37%, SNDK -37%), with **CPUs down -21% as a sub-sector** since the SOX peak and the SOX itself -19% off its high. **The framing that makes the print tractable: UBS's 07-25 cut from $470 to $360 was a PEG re-rating (2.4x → 1.8x) driven *entirely* by handsets — DC forecasts were left untouched and C2030 AGI revenue was actually RAISED to ~$14B — so a beat has to come from the DC and AGI lines, not from royalty rates.** ⚠️ **And the counter-datapoint landed 24 hours BEFORE that cut and is still unreconciled: [[QCOM]] is reported (Bloomberg, 07-24) to be telling customers prices are going up by double digits.** If handset silicon is passing memory cost through rather than absorbing it, the royalty-base assumption behind the ARM cut is more fragile than it reads — the mechanism UBS used (Apple/Samsung absorb, Chinese Android does not) survives only if the pass-through fails at the low end. Diary: **BofA (Vivek Arya) and UBS (Tim Arcuri) both hosting ARM earnings-recap calls 7/30, 8am ET.** Cross-filed [[QCOM]], [[AAPL]]. |
